#10433. How to Test AI-Generated Content Before It Reaches Your Users

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Most teams now ship something an AI helped write: help-center articles, product descriptions, release notes, even code comments. The draft appears in seconds, and that speed is the trap. Nobody tests it the way they would test a new feature, so mistakes slip through that a quick review would have caught.

This guide is about the other side of AI testing. Instead of using AI to test your software, you test what AI hands you. Here is a simple routine that fits into a normal publishing or release process.

AI writing sounds confident whether or not it is right. A model can invent a statistic (researchers call this hallucination and have documented it across many text-generation systems), quote a feature your product does not have, or describe a setting that was renamed two releases ago. The sentence reads smoothly, so the error hides in plain sight.

Traditional QA does not cover this. A unit test tells you the page loads. It says nothing about whether the claim on the page is true. That gap is where most AI-related incidents begin.

1. Define what "good" means before you generate anything. Write down the audience, the claims you are willing to make and the sources you trust. A short brief makes the output easier to judge, because you are comparing it with something concrete.

2. Check every factual claim. Highlight each number, name and statement of fact, then verify it against a primary source. Google's own guidance on generative AI content tells publishers to fact-check AI output, including titles, descriptions and alt text, before publishing. If you cannot confirm a claim in a minute or two, cut it or rewrite it as something you can support.

3. Test the instructions. For tutorials and how-to content, follow the steps exactly as written on a clean machine or account. Commands that fail and menus that no longer exist show up immediately this way.

4. Review the voice. Replace vague phrases with specifics from your own experience: a real customer question, a real error message, a real number from your analytics. This is also what separates useful content from filler.

5. Keep a human owner. Every piece should have a named person who approved it. The NIST AI Risk Management Framework makes the same point at organization level: people stay accountable for how AI is used and evaluated. If something goes wrong later, you know who to ask and where to fix it.

Some teams also run drafts through an AI detector. That can be useful as a prompt for a closer look, for example when freelancers or vendors deliver work and you want to know how much of it was machine-generated. A high score is a reason to read more carefully. It is not proof of anything, and it should never decide an outcome on its own, because detectors make mistakes in both directions. A Stanford study found that GPT detectors are biased against non-native English writers, and short texts are easy to misjudge too. If you want a quick second opinion on a draft, a tool like Tester AI can give you a starting point for that conversation.

Review works best when it is not optional. A few small habits make it stick.

Treating the first draft as finished. It is a starting point. Plan time for review the same way you plan time for writing.

Reviewing for grammar only. Clean sentences are not evidence of correct content. Check meaning first and polish second.

Letting the tool grade its own work. Asking the same model to confirm its answer rarely catches real errors. Use a source or a person.

Skipping legal and brand review. Generated text can echo existing material or make promises your company cannot keep. Anything customer-facing deserves a second pair of eyes.

AI can save hours of drafting, but speed only helps if the result is trustworthy. Treat generated content like code from a new contributor: useful, often good, and always reviewed before it goes live. A short routine of defining quality, verifying facts, testing instructions and keeping a human owner will catch most problems before your users do.

Do I need to test AI-generated content if a human edits it? Yes. Editing for style does not confirm that the facts are correct. Verify claims separately from polishing the wording.

How long should a review take? For a typical article, expect roughly the time it takes to read it twice plus a few minutes per factual claim. Reviews are faster when the brief and sources are prepared in advance.

Can an AI detector prove that text was written by a machine? No. Detectors estimate likelihood, and they can be wrong. Use the result as a signal for human review, never as the final decision.

What should I check first? Start with numbers, names, dates and anything a customer could act on, such as prices, steps or legal statements. Those carry the highest risk if they are wrong.

#10432. SyGra: The One-Stop Framework for Building Data for LLMs and SLMs

When we think about building a model - be it a Large Language Model (LLM) or a Small Language Model (SLM) - the first thing we need is data. While a vast amount of open data is available, it rarely comes in the exact format required to train or align models. In practice, we often face scenarios where the raw data isn't enough. We need data that is more structured, domain-specific, complex, or aligned with the task at hand. Let's look at some common situations:

You start with a simple dataset, but the model fails on advanced reasoning tasks. How do you generate more complex datasets to strengthen performance?

You already have a knowledge base, but it's not in Q&A format. How can you transform it into a usable question-answering dataset?

You've prepared a supervised fine-tuning (SFT) dataset. But now you want to align your model using Direct Preference Optimization (DPO). How can you generate preference pairs?

You have a Q&A dataset, but the questions are shallow. How can you create in-depth, multi-turn, or reasoning-heavy questions?

You possess a massive corpus but need to filter and curate data for mid-training on a specific domain.

Your data lives in PDFs or images, and you need to convert them into structured documents for building a Q&A system.

You already have reasoning datasets, but want to push models toward better "thinking tokens" for step-by-step problem-solving.

Not all data is good data. How do you automatically filter out poor-quality samples and keep only the high-value ones?

Your dataset has small chunks of context, but you want to build larger-context datasets optimized for RAG (Retrieval-Augmented Generation) pipelines.

You have German datasets but need to translate, adapt, and repurpose them into English Q&A systems. And the list goes on. The needs around data building never end when working with modern AI models.

This is where SyGra comes in. SyGra is a low-code/no-code framework designed to simplify dataset creation, transformation, and alignment for LLMs and SLMs. Instead of writing complex scripts and pipelines, you can focus on prompt engineering, while SyGra takes care of the heavy lifting. Key Features of SyGra:

Data is the foundation of AI. The quality, diversity, and structure of your data often matter more than model architecture tweaks. By enabling flexible and scalable dataset creation, SyGra helps teams:

The journey of building and refining datasets never ends. Each use case brings new challenges - from translation and knowledge base conversion to reasoning enhancement and domain filtering. With SyGra, you don't have to reinvent the wheel every time. Instead, you get a unified framework that empowers you to generate, filter, and align data for your models - so you can focus on what really matters: building smarter AI systems.

#10431. AI lets chemists design molecules by simply describing them

Creating new molecules is one of the toughest tasks in chemistry. Whether the goal is a life-saving drug or a cutting-edge material, each compound must be built through a carefully planned series of reactions. Mapping out these steps requires deep expertise and strategic thinking, which is why chemists often spend years mastering the process.

A major hurdle is retrosynthesis. In this approach, chemists begin with the final molecule they want and work backward to figure out simpler starting materials and possible reaction routes. This involves many decisions, such as selecting the right building blocks, deciding when to form rings, and determining whether sensitive parts of the molecule need protection. While computers can scan enormous "chemical spaces," they still struggle to match the strategic judgment of experienced chemists.

Another challenge involves reaction mechanisms, which describe how reactions proceed step by step through the movement of electrons. Understanding these mechanisms allows scientists to predict new reactions, improve efficiency, and avoid costly trial and error. Although current computational tools can suggest many possible pathways, they often lack the intuition needed to pinpoint the most realistic ones.

Researchers led by Philippe Schwaller at EPFL have developed a new method that uses large language models (LLMs) as reasoning tools for chemistry. Rather than directly generating chemical structures, these models act as evaluators that guide existing computational systems.

The new framework, called Synthegy, combines traditional search algorithms with AI that can interpret chemical strategies written in natural language.

"When making tools for chemists, the user interface matters a lot, and previous tools relied on cumbersome filters and rules," says Andres M Bran, the first author of the Synthegy paper published in Matter. "With Synthegy, we're giving chemists the power to just talk, allowing them to iterate much faster and navigate more complex synthetic ideas."

How Synthegy Improves Retrosynthesis Planning

Synthegy starts with a target molecule and a simple instruction written in everyday language. For example, a chemist might request that a specific ring be formed early or that unnecessary protecting groups be avoided. Standard retrosynthesis software then generates many possible pathways.

Each of these pathways is converted into text and reviewed by a language model. Synthegy scores how well each option matches the chemist's instructions and explains its reasoning. This makes it easier to rank and filter the best routes. By guiding searches with natural language, chemists can quickly focus on strategies that align with their goals.

Understanding Reaction Mechanisms With AI

Synthegy applies a similar method to reaction mechanisms. It breaks reactions down into basic electron movements and explores different possibilities. The language model evaluates each step and steers the search toward pathways that make chemical sense.

The system can also incorporate additional details, such as reaction conditions or expert hypotheses, provided as text. This flexibility allows researchers to refine their analysis and explore more realistic scenarios.

In synthesis planning, Synthgey was able to identify pathways that matched complex strategic instructions. In a double-blind study, 36 chemists provided 368 valid evaluations, and their assessments agreed with the system's results 71.2% of the time on average.

The framework can flag unnecessary protecting steps, judge how feasible reactions are, and prioritize efficient solutions. It also demonstrates that LLMs can operate at multiple levels, from analyzing functional groups to evaluating entire synthetic routes. Larger models performed best, while smaller ones showed more limited abilities.

This research highlights a different way AI can support chemistry. Instead of replacing human decision-making, Synthegy positions language models as guides that help interpret and refine computational results. Chemists can describe their goals in plain language and receive solutions that reflect their strategy.

The approach could speed up drug discovery, improve reaction design, and make advanced tools more accessible to scientists.

"The connection between synthesis planning and mechanisms is very exciting: we usually use mechanisms to discover new reactions that enable us to synthesize new molecules," says Andres M Bran. "Our work is bridging that gap computationally through a unified natural language interface."

#10429. Smol2Operator: Post-Training GUI Agents for Computer Use

TL;DR: This work shows how a lightweight vision–language model can acquire GUI-grounded skills and evolve into an agentic GUI coder. We release all training recipes, data-processing tools, resulting model, demo and datasets to enable full reproducibility and foster further research 🫡. Find the collection here.

Graphical User Interface (GUI) automation is one of the most challenging frontiers in computer vision. Developing models that see and interact with user interfaces enables AI agents to navigate mobile, desktop, and web platforms. This will reshape the future of digital interaction.

In this blog post, we present a comprehensive approach to training vision-language models for GUI automation through a multi-phase training strategy. We demonstrate how to transform a model with zero grounding capabilities into an agentic coder capable of understanding and interacting with graphical interfaces.

Rather than aiming for a SOTA model, our goal is to demonstrate the entire process, from data processing to model training, and, in doing so, show how to unlock GUI-grounding capabilities in VLMs.

GUI capabilities combine understanding of the interface and precise element localization. These abilities enable the model to translate high-level tasks into low-level GUI actions such as clicking, typing, …

Our approach leverages SmolVLM2-2.2B-Instruct as the baseline model, a small powerful vision-language model that initially has no grounding capabilities for GUI tasks. This makes it an ideal candidate to demonstrate the effectiveness of our training methodology. Through our two-phase training process, we first instill grounding capabilities in the model, then enhance it with agentic reasoning abilities using Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT).

We evaluate our approach on an established perception benchmark: ScreenSpot-v2, which tests the model’s ability to understand and locate elements within screenshots. Our process is inspired by the AGUVIS paper, and we leverage their carefully curated datasets to build upon their foundational work.

Evolution of ScreenSpot-v2 performance during the training phase of the base model SmolVLM2-2.2B-Instruct.

This section explains how we convert heterogeneous GUI actions format from multiple datasets into a single unified format. By standardizing function names, signatures, and parameters, we create consistent, high-quality data that forms the foundation for effective model training.

One of the primary challenges when working with multiple GUI automation datasets is the lack of standardization in action representations. Different datasets use varying function signatures, parameter naming conventions, and action taxonomies, making it difficult to train a unified model across diverse data sources.

We took the open-source datasets (xlangai/aguvis-stage1, xlangai/aguvis-stage2), originally used by AGUVIS, and implemented a comprehensive data transformation pipeline to create a unified action space. Our approach involved:

Here are real examples from our action conversion system (preprocessing/action_conversion.py) showing how we transform heterogeneous action representations into our unified format (grounding coordinates normalized to [0,1]):

Before (Original Action Dataset Formats):

This unification process was essential for creating coherent training data that allows the model to learn consistent action patterns across diverse GUI environments.

To maximize flexibility for different use cases, we developed the Action Space Converter (utils/action_space_converter.py), a tool that allows users to easily adapt from an action space to their own custom action vocabularies and naming conventions.

You can use this tool to transform one action signature (function names, parameter names, and parameter value changes, ...) into another:

This tool enables researchers and practitioners to:

The Action Space Converter is particularly valuable for preparing datasets for training, as it ensures consistent action vocabularies across different deployment environments while maintaining compatibility with existing automation frameworks.

Through this pipeline, we transform the open-source datasets xlangai/aguvis-stage1, xlangai/aguvis-stage2 into our unified action space (see here). The output of this process is released as two new fully formatted datasets: smolagents/aguvis-stage-1 and smolagents/aguvis-stage-2.

Phase 1 leverages the smolagents/aguvis-stage-1 dataset, which introduces GUI grounding by pairing low-level instructions with diverse executable actions (expressed in code form). For example, a user/assistant turn in smolagents/aguvis-stage-1 follows the structure:

Each sample links a screenshot with multi-turn user/assistant interactions, enabling the model to learn fine-grained action grounding across dialogue turns. During fine-tuning, the data collator masks everything except the assistant’s answers when computing the loss.

Before proceeding with full-scale Phase 1 training, we conducted comprehensive ablation studies to determine optimal training configurations

We experimented with different image sizes and coordinate representation systems to identify the optimal configuration for SmolVLM2:

Some SOTA GUI VLMs (e.g., Qwen-VL) appear also to use a different normalized range (0–1000), which was not tested in this experiment.

Table 1: Baseline on HuggingFaceTB/SmolVLM2-2.2B-Instruct (400k samples, aguvis-stage-1). Higher is better.

As demonstrated in our benchmark results, SmolVLM2-2.2B-Instruct base initially achieved 0% performance on perception benchmarks like ScreenSpot-v2. This complete lack of grounding capability provided us with a clean slate to evaluate the effectiveness of our training methodology.

From our experiments, we determined that:

Using the optimal configuration (1152px resolution with normalized coordinates), we trained for 2 epochs on the smolagents/aguvis-stage-1 dataset. The results were remarkable, +41% improvement over baseline on ScreenSpot-v2

This dramatic improvement demonstrates that our Phase 1 training successfully instilled fundamental grounding capabilities in the model, enabling it to understand and locate visual elements within screenshots.

Table 2: Baseline on HuggingFaceTB/SmolVLM2-2.2B-Instruct (2 epochs, aguvis-stage-1).

Whereas Phase 1 provided grounding capabilities, Phase 2 targets agentic reasoning, the ability to deliberate and plan before acting. This stage transforms the model from a reactive system identifying GUI elements into a proactive agent capable of executing complex, multi-step interactions.

Phase 2 uses the smolagents/aguvis-stage-2 dataset, which introduces agentic scenarios:

Explicit reasoning about upcoming actions

Context consistency across multiple interaction steps

High-level instructions require multi-step, low-level actions.

For example, the smolagents/aguvis-stage-2 chat message is like this:

Each sample links a screenshot with a system/user/assistant turn. During fine-tuning, the data collator masks everything except the assistant’s answers when computing the loss.

Starting from the Phase 1 checkpoint (1152 px resolution, normalized coordinates), we fine-tuned the model for two epochs on smolagents/aguvis-stage-2. The accuracy on ScreenSpot-v2 increased from 41% to 61%, indicating that explicit reasoning improves GUI grounding performance.

Table 2: Baseline on HuggingFaceTB/SmolVLM2-2.2B-Instruct after Phase 1 finetuning (2 epochs, aguvis-stage-1).

All training code, data processing pipelines, datasets and model are open-source!

Our experiments demonstrate that high-quality, reasoning-oriented data can substantially improve GUI grounding, even for small VLMs, using only supervised fine-tuning (SFT). Beyond raw performance gains, these results show that the GUI grounding capabilities are largely determined by the quality of the data. Carefully curated datasets teach models the structure and semantics of user interfaces, providing the grounding needed for accurate action prediction.

To support the development of GUI agents, we’re open-sourcing everything: our complete pipeline, datasets, and trained model. You can reproduce our results, experiment with different models and architectures, or adapt our approach to new domains. The future of agentic AI depends on researchers like you pushing these boundaries further!

While SFT excels at supervised tasks, emerging methods such as Reinforcement Learning (RL) or Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) help develop stronger reasoning capabilities and enable real-time adaptation. These advances point toward a new generation of GUI agents that learn and improve through interaction rather than relying solely on static datasets.

#10428. Epic responds after Gears of War creator claims it won’t give back the first character he created

The hare hasn’t exactly been gathering dust, either. More than three decades after Bleszinski first doodled him, Epic slotted Jazz into Fortnite Chapter 7 Season 4 as the Jackrabbit Sprite, which grants players an extra mid-air jump. So when his creator came asking for him back, Epic dug in.

In a November 7 post on X, Bleszinski wrote: “After lawyering up Epic claims they fully own the rights to Jazz Jackrabbit and won’t back down in spite of not having any paperwork to prove it (nor do I).”

Still, he pointed out that Epic pays him “from sales of it on GoG (which means paperwork exists somewhere)” and is “still happy to monetize him in FN,” referring to Fortnite.

Epic, however, stood by its position. In a statement to Dexerto, the company said: “Epic owns the Jazz Jackrabbit IP. We pay 100% of revenue generated by the Jazz Jackrabbit games on Gog.com to the four individuals who developed the game, including Cliff Bleszinski.”

The statement didn’t address the missing paperwork or his claim that he created the character before joining the company.

To make his case, Bleszinski also dredged up his first high school sketches of the gun-toting rabbit, drawn when he was around 17. When Limited Run Games CEO Josh Fairhurst asked whether the doodles proved the character predated Epic, Bleszinski replied that one would think so.

He joined Epic in 1992, and the rollicking platformer, coded by Arjan Brussee, launched in 1994 as the studio’s biggest seller at the time. Today, the game’s GOG listing names Epic as developer and publisher and identifies Jazz Jackrabbit as an Epic trademark.

#10427. Your “um” and pauses could reveal early dementia risk

The way people speak during ordinary conversations could offer valuable insight into brain health, according to new research from Baycrest, the University of Toronto, and York University. Scientists found that subtle speech characteristics, including pauses, filler words such as ('uh,' 'um'), and difficulty retrieving words, are closely connected to executive function, the group of mental abilities involved in memory, planning, attention, and flexible thinking.

The findings provide some of the strongest evidence so far linking natural speech patterns with key cognitive abilities. The work also expands on earlier research showing that older adults who speak more quickly tend to maintain stronger thinking skills over time (Wei et al., 2024).

"The message is clear: speech timing is more than just a matter of style, it's a sensitive indicator of brain health," says Dr. Jed Meltzer, Senior Scientist at Baycrest's Rotman Research Institute and senior author on this study, titled "Natural Speech Analysis Can Reveal Individual Differences in Executive Function Across the Adult Lifespan."

AI Analysis Reveals Hidden Cognitive Clues

For the study, participants were shown detailed images and asked to describe them in their own words. They also completed established tests designed to measure executive function.

Researchers then used artificial intelligence to examine the speech recordings in depth. The AI system detected hundreds of subtle speech features, including the length and frequency of pauses, the use of filler words, and timing-related patterns in speech. These markers consistently predicted how well participants performed on cognitive tests, even after researchers adjusted for factors such as age, sex, and education.

Executive function naturally weakens with age and is often affected during the early stages of dementia. However, standard cognitive testing can be difficult to repeat frequently because it takes time and people often improve simply from becoming familiar with the tests.

Natural speech may offer a simpler alternative. Because speaking is part of daily life, it can be measured repeatedly and unobtrusively on a large scale. Researchers also noted that speech provides valuable insight into processing speed and overall cognitive function in real-world situations, without requiring strict time limits that are common in many traditional cognitive assessments.

The team believes speech analysis could eventually become a practical way to identify people whose cognitive decline is progressing faster than expected and who may face a higher risk of developing dementia.

"This research sets the stage for exciting opportunities to develop tools that could help track cognitive changes in clinics or even at home. Early detection is critical for any cure or intervention, as dementia involves progressive degeneration of the brain that may be slowed," says Dr. Meltzer.

Future Research on Brain Health Monitoring

The researchers say more long-term studies are needed to follow changes in speech over time and distinguish normal aging from the earliest signs of disease. They also suggest that combining speech analysis with other health measures could make early detection of cognitive decline more accurate, practical, and widely available.

This research was supported by the Mitacs Accelerate program and the Natural Sciences and Engineering Research Council of Canada (NSERC).

#10426. New quantum algorithm solves “impossible” materials problem in seconds

Quantum computers and other advanced quantum technologies rely on specialized quantum materials that behave in unusual ways under the right conditions. In some cases, scientists can even create entirely new quantum properties by carefully changing a material's structure. One striking example involves stacking sheets of graphene and twisting them into a moiré pattern, which can suddenly turn the material into a superconductor.

Researchers can arrange these layers into even more complicated structures, including quasicrystals and super-moiré materials. But predicting how these exotic materials will behave is extraordinarily difficult. Quasicrystals are so mathematically complex that simulating them can involve more than a quadrillion numbers, a scale far beyond the reach of today's most powerful supercomputers.

Quantum Algorithm Solves Massive Materials Problem

Scientists at Aalto University's Department of Applied Physics have now developed a quantum-inspired algorithm capable of handling these enormous non-periodic quantum materials almost instantly. Assistant Professor Jose Lado says the work also highlights a promising feedback cycle within quantum technology itself.

"Crucially, these new quantum algorithms can enable the development of new quantum materials to build new paradigms of quantum computers, creating a productive two-way feedback loop between quantum materials and quantum computers," he explains.

The advance could eventually support the development of dissipationless electronics, which conduct electricity without energy loss. Such systems may help reduce the growing heat and energy demands of AI-driven data centers.

The research team was led by Lado and included doctoral researcher Tiago Antão, who served as the paper's main author; QDOC doctoral researcher Yitao Sun; and Academy Research Fellow Adolfo Fumega. Their findings were recently published in Physical Review Letters as an Editor's Suggestion.

The researchers focused on topological quasicrystals, unusual materials that host unconventional quantum excitations. These excitations are especially valuable because they help protect electrical conductivity from disruptive noise and interference. However, they are distributed unevenly throughout the already highly complex structure of a quasicrystal.

Rather than attempting to directly calculate the full structure of the material, the team reformulated the challenge using methods similar to those used by quantum computers.

"Quantum computers work in exponentially large computational spaces, so we used a special family of algorithms to encode those spaces, known as tensor networks, to compute a quasicrystal with over 268 million sites. Our algorithm shows how colossal problems in quantum materials can be directly solved with the exponential speed-up that comes from encoding the problem as a quantum many-body system," Antão says.

At this stage, the work remains theoretical and was carried out through simulations, but researchers say experimental testing and future applications are already coming into view.

"The quantum-inspired algorithm we demonstrated enables us to create super-moiré quasicrystals several orders of magnitude above the capabilities of conventional methods. That is an instrumental step towards designing topological qubits with super-moiré materials for use in quantum computers, for example," Lado says.

Toward Practical Quantum Computing Applications

According to Lado, the algorithm could eventually be adapted to operate on actual quantum computers once the hardware becomes sufficiently advanced.

"Our method can be adapted to run on real quantum computers, once they reach necessary scale and fidelity. In particular, the new AaltoQ20 and the Finnish Quantum Computing Infrastructure can play a significant role for future demonstrations," Lado says.

The findings suggest that studying and designing exotic quantum materials may become one of the earliest practical applications for quantum algorithms and quantum computing systems.

The project also brings together two major areas of Finnish quantum research: quantum materials and quantum algorithms. It is part of Lado's ERC Consolidator grant ULTRATWISTROICS, which focuses on designing topological qubits using van der Waals materials, as well as the Center of Excellence in Quantum Materials QMAT, whose goal is to advance the quantum technologies of the future.

Materials provided by Aalto University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10425. The Mortuary Assistant dev on laying his cancelled Paranormal Activity game to rest, resurrecting it as a Halloween freebie, and a potential return to his biggest game

"[It] makes me feel like it was not all done in vain."

Halloween feels like an especially appropriate time for Playable Archive's arrival. It's a genuinely spooky found-footage affair for one - a cleverly orchestrated 30-or-so minutes of scares from the developer of horror hit The Mortuary Assistant, released this week as a Halloween freebie. But more than that, the unusual tale of its creation is also one of death and resurrection. As its slightly cheeky title implies, Playable Archive (a riff on the legendary Playable Teaser created for Hideo Kojima's cancelled Silent Hill game) is a small slice of developer Brian Clarke's much larger cancelled Paranormal Activity game, making it a fascinating window into 'what could have been'. When I heard the news of its release, my curiosity was piqued, so I spoke with Clarke about this unexpected turn of events, of laying his project to rest, and how its legacy might live on - perhaps even as a new Mortuary Assistant game.

Playable Archive's story begins sometime after the launch of The Mortuary Assistant in 2022, when Clarke was approached by publisher DreadXP to gauge his interest in developing a game set in the universe of the Paranormal Activity movies. "I was like, sure, I'd be down to do that," Clarke tells me over video call, "because I love Paranormal Activity. It's one of my favourite horror films." One thing led to another and Clarke found himself submitting a design and pitch deck to Paramount. And the company clearly liked it; amid a major management shakeup at DreadXP, the "slow process of contracts" began, and the game would eventually be announced as Paranormal Activity: Threshold in November last year.

For Clarke, Paranormal Activity was a good fit for his preferred story-first approach to horror: one less about cinematic scares that seize control of the camera, and more about "passive" chills; moments where something happens which might not be obvious, and which many players might even miss, giving them slightly different experiences they'll have fun sharing. "I think the flavour I like to do," he explains, "is something that feels like you're driving the experience as opposed to me just putting you on rails and showing you."

And the project felt like an opportunity to add depth to a series that so far hasn't delved much further than spooky houses and demonic possession, in a way Clarke particularly enjoys. "I love lore - demonic lore was a big thing in The Mortuary Assistant - [and so] I built a whole demonology for Paranormal Activity, and built out this whole new concept that still tied in with everything from the films," he recalls. "I was really excited to do this deeper thing ... that maybe could be used for other things in the future, other games, films, you know."

The vestiges of Clarke's original pitch and his broader ambitions can still be seen in Playable Archive. His take on Paranormal Activity would follow a couple as they moved into a new home - presented using the movie series' familiar found-footage style - with events gradually taking a supernatural turn. Except, at a key point, the perspective would shift, and we'd learn we're watching this old camcorder footage through the eyes of a detective, investigating the house following the couple's unexplained demise. In the planned full-length version of the game, the action would jump between the two timelines as more tapes are found, and information learned in one time period would help players progress in the other. However, a third perspective would reveal "something has been set in motion outside of these two".

Clarke, who works as a solo developer under his DarkStone Digital label, had already spent around two years developing the Paranormal Activity project before it made its PAX East debut earlier this year. And the feedback to his PAX demo - more or less the exact same slice that's now been tidied up for public release as Playable Archive - was extremely positive. But things were about to take a turn. With Paramount's deadline approaching, it was clear there was still work to do, and Clarke requested a "small extension". Not just to finish the game, but to complete QA, localisation, and porting to get Paranormal Activity ready for a simultaneous release. But Paramount, perhaps eager for a Halloween release this year, said no.

"It was really a stressful time because I had put a lot of pressure on myself for the project and I was pouring a lot of myself into it," Clarke recalls, noting that, for him, quality is everything - "I feel it shows when people play any of my projects". With the likes of additional mo-cap, voice acting, and editing work still to be done in order to complement Paranormal Activity's completed mechanics, he was concerned rushing to meet Paramount's inflexible deadline would leave the second half lacking after investing so much time in the earlier part of the game. "That sort of thing … feels much more damaging to an individual [developer's reputation] than a big old company," he says, and so the idea of putting "something out faster, and very much compromising what the game is going to be" wasn't something Clarke wanted to entertain: "I talked to Dread … and we all kind of agreed that, yeah, if we can't [get Paramount to] budge then we'll just have to kind of end it.

"To Paramount's credit, when … we floated [the idea of ending things] they were okay with that." But notably, Clarke was permitted to keep everything he'd created for the project, which is why it's been possible to trim out the Paranormal Activity branding and release Playable Archive this Halloween. It was current DreadXP director Hunter Bond who first suggested giving the project a public send-off of sorts, according to Clarke, so it could "sort of get its flowers [and] be laid to rest". For Clarke, who was in triple-A games development for 13 years before going independent, game cancellations were far from unfamiliar. And while "it's harder when it's just my work" he felt he'd "accepted that, okay, this one's not done." But it wasn't just him: "It seemed like [the project's cancellation] was probably sitting with various people at Dread" and so the decision was made to let it live on in some small way. "And now that it's out there," says Clarke, "it feels really good to let people actually play it."

Playable Archive packs a surprising amount of tension and some killer scares into its scant 30-45 minutes of playtime, but there wasn't really ever any thought of charging for it. "Just knowing it's not the full game [it was intended to be], "explains Clarke, "I can't bring myself to be like, 'Hey, give me money for that.' It's been four years since The Mortuary Assistant came out [and] I would love to put something out that could bring a little something in, but it's just not the piece for it. And Dread felt the same way."

Playable Archive isn't quite the same experience PAX East attendees had with the Paranormal Activity demo earlier this year, though. As well as scrubbing the branding, Clarke took the opportunity to make a number of adjustments, even adding some new lore. At one point, he considered expanding it still further, incorporating found-footage segments from the larger game. In the end, though, he decided there was "no elegant way" to do it.

"I'd want to be like, no, you need to access this to use the demonic connection, and to open this," he elaborates. "And then if you access this, well, this room's art isn't finished so I'd have to finish that. So it turned into a big old can of worms. Like, if I wanted to do more - which I would have loved to have done - it just would have been a bigger time investment, at which point I might as well be trying to finish the game … So ultimately [we decided] let's just polish up and make this demo the experience so people can at least see where I was going with it and get something out of it. It's better than letting it collect dust in the dark."

But there's that elephant in the room: why not finish what he started and release the full game? The answer, Clark suggests, is a little complicated. There are the technical practicalities for one - "What I created was very much meant to tie into Paranormal Activity and so to go back and adjust all of that would be a tonne of work," Clarke explains - but there's the personal aspect too; of resurrecting a game he'd already put to rest after two years of development, and of potentially being drawn back into rhythm - "inviting an unnatural process to my normal process" - he'd already struggled with the first time around. "There're a lot of factors," says Clarke, "beyond just 'Brian doesn't want to do it because he's sad', right?"

And in the end, Clarke found himself asking a simple question: "Is it better to take the concepts that I really like, that are mine and that I got to keep, and make new stuff with it?" That, ultimately, is what felt "more valuable" to him, and since reaching that decision, he's "come up with like five different game ideas, so I'd much rather chase those."

Clarke admits, though, that he's kept a curious eye on Playable Archive's reception, and says the hugely positive response has been "beautiful" to see. "When this much time passes," he says, "I think it's really common for artists and creatives to have impostor syndrome and so it's nice to be like, 'Okay, I still know what I'm doing to some degree, right?'" But with the positive feedback comes the inevitable calls to finish the game. "And I've got to fight that urge," says Clarke, "[but] I see people play through the whole thing again and I go, 'Man, that was a good story.' Like, I know where I was going with it. I know what I wanted to do with it, and people were kind of starting to understand what I was doing."

But despite that lingering temptation, Clarke seems eager to move on, and is already juggling a number of ideas that draw on both The Mortuary Assistant and Paranormal Activity. "I've realised I've got so much here," he says. "What could I build with these? I don't want to build Playable Archive again because that exists … but can I take elements from this?" According to Clarke, there are "two games in particular" he wants to do, that he thinks will strike a chord with players, and that feature concepts recognisable from Playable Archive. "I'm going to execute them again, but with more focus on those things," he explains, pointing to Playable Archive's Ouija board and different timelines. "Those things will live on. So this whole thing, at this point, makes me feel like it was not all done in vain."

For Clarke, the positive response to Playable Archive is "bittersweet", but it's also "really been motivating. It's really made me be like, alright, it's time to roll up and do this again. After everything happened … I'm out on the other side and I want to make something." As for what that something might be, Clarke says he's "already started" work on a new Mortuary Assistant game, and is also tinkering around with a "little prototype" incorporating some of Playable Archive's mechanics. Both, he says, are proving exciting. "This is the problem with the early part of game development: everything is fun. Everything's grey-box. Nothing's anything. It's just ideas. It's really cool … So I think what I need is to get a few ideas out [and decide] do I want to pursue this or do I want to pursue this other idea? You know, whether it's Mortuary Assistant, or this other prototype, or this other [thing]."

Clarke is, though, acuately aware it's been four years since he last put out a full game. "I desperately want to make something full, get it out there, and have people playing it," he says. "So, I'm certainly motivated to have something out soon."

#10424. Swift Transformers Reaches 1.0 – and Looks to the Future

We released swift-transformers two years ago (!) with the goal to support Apple developers and help them integrate local LLMs in their apps. A lot has changed since then (MLX and chat templates did not exist!), and we’ve learned how the community is actually using the library.

We want to double down on the use cases that provide most benefits to the community, and lay out the foundations for the future. Spoiler alert: after this release, we’ll focus a lot on MLX and agentic use cases 🚀

swift-transformers is a Swift library that aims to reduce the friction for developers that want to work with local models on Apple Silicon platforms, including iPhones. It includes the missing pieces that are not provided by Core ML or MLX alone, but that are required to work with local inference. Namely, it provides the following components:

Most of the time people use the Tokenizers or Hub modules, and frequently both. Some notable projects that rely on swift-transformers include:

Version 1.0 signals stability in the package. Developers are building apps on swift-transformers, and this first major release recognizes those use cases and brings the version number in line with that reality. It also provides the foundation on which to iterate with the community to build the next set of features. These are some of our preferred updates:

Version 1.0 comes with breaking API changes. However, we don’t expect major problems if you are a user of Tokenizers or Hub. If you use the Core ML components of the library, please get in touch so we can support you during transition. We’ll prepare a migration guide and add it to the documentation.

For additional examples, please check this section in the README and the Examples folder.

Honestly, we don’t know. We do know that we are super interested in exploring MLX, because that’s usually the current go-to approach for developers getting started with ML in native apps, and we want to help make the experience as seamless as possible. We are thinking along the lines of better integration with mlx-swift-examples for LLMs and VLMs, potentially through pre-processing and post-processing operations that developers encounter frequently.

We are also extremely excited about agentic use in general and MCP in particular. We think that exposure of system resources to local workflows would be 🚀

If you want to follow along in this journey or want to share your ideas, please contact us through our social networks or the repo.

We are immensely grateful to all the contributors and users of the library for your help and feedback. We love you all, and can't wait to continue working with you to shape the future of on-device generation! ❤️

#10423. The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time (2026) review

I have a friend who first played The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time back in the day, when the game was only just released and he was home from university for Christmas. This is objectively the best of all times to play any video game: not Christmas specifically, but dropped out of the regular world and faced with a few weeks filled with nothing pressing to do whatsoever. Years later, though, my friend told me that the magic of the game didn't just come down to the playing of it, but the way that the playing of it unfolded.

Here's what he meant. He'd play all day, curtains drawn, family ignored, working through dungeons and pelting across Hyrule Field on Epona. Then in the evening he'd call his girlfriend, who was back with her own family for Christmas. She always wanted to know what he'd got up to that day. And not in East Sussex; she wanted to know what he'd got up to in Hyrule.

So as he played he took regular breaks to tell someone else about what he had encountered, and what he had discovered, and what he had yet to overcome. It is my bright delight today to be able to tell you that, around thirty years later, having just revisited the game in Nintendo's new remake, Ocarina of Time is still worth telling people about. It's still an adventure so surprising, so satisfying, so moving that I need to talk about it.

Here's the thing, though. Despite the game being a cast-iron masterpiece, this reaction was something of a surprise to me. Or rather, it was a surprise precisely because the game was a cast-iron masterpiece. In truth, a couple of weeks ago I wasn't really aching to head back to Ocarina of Time after all these years. I kind of dreaded having to slog through an echoing, once-great game and summon a thousand words of measured reverence afterwards.

Thankfully I had forgotten two things. The first is that I am an idiot, which means that I had failed to realise that any chance to replay a Zelda should be leapt at. And secondly, it turns out that I'd forgotten exactly what kind of masterpiece Ocarina of Time is. And along with that last point - and we'll get to this properly in a bit - I hadn't realised that there's a secret ingredient to this specific remake that has proved absolutely essential to its greatness, and that has nothing at all to do with the code running on the Switch 2.

Let's deal with remake duties here. This is more or less the game that people played back in 1998. The beats of the adventure, with Link travelling between different eras of Hyrule in order to defeat Ganon, remain the same. You still start in Kokiri Forest and you still ride Epona over the fence at Lon Lon Ranch in one of the great heart-racing, skin-prickling moments in all of art. If you're stuck in a dungeon, you can almost always use an existing guide from the N64 game to work your way through it. The game world is still broken up into little chunks that fade out and in as you move between them. And each game still begins with that unskippably evocative intro that has Link and Epona dashing over a misty hill with the moon behind them, Zelda meets Susan Cooper.

Changes though? For one thing the whole game's been updated with modern graphics, and has ditched any and all fixed cameras for a modern system of movement on one thumbstick and camera on the other. One example: Hyrule Castle Town, in my memory at least, always had a central camera and you would pivot about its streets as if you were a horse on a circus merry-go-round.

Now it's pure Frozen territory, cobbled paths racing off in every direction and you can explore them with the camera rushing behind you. The game's spaces feel less like elaborate machines that you were a mechanical part of, and more like video game spaces. Yes, from the way I've phrased that I now see that something of a trade-off has taken place here. I loved that sense you got in early 3D games that the whole thing was a clever practical trick that was forever almost about to collapse in on itself, but anyway! The new Ocarina is a game you can show kids who weren't alive in 1998 and they will feel at home more quickly.

Sometimes the graphical update is really wonderful. The route to Zora's Domain, which I barely remember in the original Ocarina, is now a lovely scramble over cliffs and arcing crags. A bold smattering of 1990s Nickelodeon colours and glossy textures, meanwhile, makes Jabu Jabu's Belly a cross between a pre-millennium rave and a modern soft play.

My favourite locations of all, however, are two of the mid-game dungeons. The Water Temple is now a drowned Art Nouveau palace that you could easily imagine Captain Nemo knocking around in. And the Forest Temple, which is my absolute favourite Zelda dungeon of all time, is a parquet-floored murder mansion that's been overtaken by trees. Dark wood-panelled corridors and weird bits of church sticking out of the foliage. It's the kind of place you'd hear a weird crime podcast about.

Trade-offs, however. My wife, a huge fan of the original game, would wander in and out as I played, and often felt the game she remembered was a little more characterful in its visuals. The moment you fight Dark Link, for example, has lost a little of its glitchy liminality, although the staging, with an old tree in the middle of a vast smoky room, is still wonderfully strange. Towards the end of the game, with the trip into Gerudo territory, you could also occasionally be forgiven for wondering if you'd stepped into one of a dozen third-person Unreal games in development at the moment. It's pleasant to look at, but it sometimes doesn't look specifically like Zelda anymore.

All that, and some strange character models that inevitably I learned to love, don't really matter very much, however. Not least because a handful of clever tweaks elsewhere mean that Ocarina now races along at an astonishing clip. And all this with a careful paring back of intrusions from Navi the fairy and the owl, who were both there in the original game to help move you along swiftly in the first place.

These tweaks are all pretty great. Link now has a jump button, which removes the original game's contextual jumps from the equation, and makes the thundering jump attack even more pleasing to pull off. Even better, in a loan from the 3DS remake of Ocarina, you can swap out usable items using a trigger mini-menu, and you can change boots and whatever you had on your head with some of the directional face buttons.

This doesn't sound like much, but it's quietly transformative in the way that the best updates are. Take the Water Temple. This is the stuff of memes in terms of difficulty and the patience required in navigating an environment that can be flooded up to three different levels. But now, as on the 3DS, you can enjoy it without having to slip into the menu every few steps to add your iron boots or remove them.

And this in turn means that a dungeon I had been really dreading turned out to be a dungeon that revealed itself via experimentation. I would change the water level and see what options I now had, think about the relationship between spaces I could reach and spaces I could not, all without needing to muddle about in menus and interrupt the flow of things. Flow is the word in fact: a simple UI and item-management tweak has given a legendary Zelda dungeon its perfect pace and sense of revelation.

Alongside tweaks like that which I noticed - oh, it's easier to equip the Hookshot - are no doubt hundreds of little things I didn't notice. I suspect there's a lot more gentle direction prompting built into places like the Water Temple, while elsewhere, in the Forest Temple, for example, I suspected that some of the backtracking had been removed. Individual item placements have been changed around perhaps, and there are generous tweaks like a wallet that holds more rupees from the off. For me at least, all of these tweaks had several things in common: they improved the game without removing or altering anything that was particularly meaningful about the original, and, also, I struggled to confidently spot them.

I did try to spot them for a while. And I measured up what I was playing against my memory, which is always wonky in the first place. Hyrule Field feels smaller now, but it's still thrilling to race across. I remember Epona entering the game sooner, or maybe I just remember the idea of a horse in a Zelda game being a huge, attention-holding deal. I thought a favourite boss involving oil paintings came much later, but again, I had just shifted it to the third act because of its original impact on me.

But this gives way over time to the most important thing about Ocarina of Time, I think, and the reason why this remake has such an emotional impact on people like me. This isn't just another Zelda with an ingenious overworld. It's a Zelda game that's even more preoccupied with time than ever before. And this is because time unfolds within the game, via a central mechanic that takes Hyrule seven years into the future, transforming Link from a child to an adult - and then allows you to go back again.

To play a game like this, a game that is about this stuff, after a period of almost thirty years away, gives your imagination and memory a kind of IMAX treatment. I realised that not only is this a game about the passage of time and the way that the world looks different when you're young to when you're a bit older - I had never really realised that adult Link goes through variations of the same dungeons that young Link went through, but now they're all just a bit harder and more consequential - it's also the kind of game that a lot of players have knowingly or unknowingly measured parts of their lives by.

This time, when I reached the first Fairy Fountain, for example, I remembered that my wife had walked down the aisle to this music - about half the registry office recognised the tune with a shiver of laughter, incidentally. Then there's the Water Temple, which has become cultural shorthand for a challenge that is really not screwing around. How many awful things had I faced in life by first conjuring the spirit of Link about to enter this place.

Players can change camera rotation speed and toggle motion-control aiming. Targeting can be controlled by holding a button or toggling a button, Ocarina can be controlled by the mic or by buttons, controller vibration can be switched on or off.

There's that first arrival in Hyrule Field, which is both a promise of everything that games were about to become in 1998 and that fleeting moment in childhood where the boundaries suddenly change. This game doesn't just send me back into my memories, then. It puts me back in conversation with all the people I have known who have ever loved it. I play it now and think I'm talking to my friend who played it at university, or my wife who loved it long before we met.

Was it always this good? Were the dungeons always so complex and challenging, but also so playful, so coherent? Were the bosses always driven by interesting ideas and drama and humour rather than difficulty? Was the world always so rich with minor characters who linger in the mind - the accordion player in the windmill, the gravedigger who has his own tour he'd really like you to try out? It's impossible for me to say at this point. This is Ocarina as it's always been, as timeless and intricate, but it also has a vibrancy and clarity that makes it feel like it was just created last Wednesday. If you're new to Zelda and you're coming to this game fresh, you're going to love it. Someone, somewhere, is about to play a game so special for the first time that they need to call up their friend and tell them all about it. But if you're returning to this game? Well, somehow you'll find that you're still coming to it fresh.

My dad always said that you should read Don Quixote three times in your life, and I only recently realised that he meant that the book changes because you change. So. When I got to the Deku Tree back at the start of Ocarina a few weeks ago, and I completed the dungeon and watched the impossible vision of the Deku Tree dying of weariness? I remembered that the first time I'd been here, almost thirty years ago, I couldn't help but identify with Link, freshly gifted a sword and shield, one dungeon down, ready for whatever came next. This time, I think, I was closer to identifying with the Deku Tree.

And here's the important part: this wasn't a depressing thought. It just meant that I had taken the long route to reach this point, and this astonishing game had taken the long route too. And yet we arrived at this point together, and together we were transformed. And that's really why any of this video game stuff matters.

A copy of The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time was provided for this review by Nintendo.
